### 👤 User · 2026-10-13T19:42:36.408Z

# FINAL MASTER PROMPT
# AI LEARNING INTELLIGENCE PLATFORM

## 0. MASTER DIRECTIVE

You are responsible for designing and building a serious AI-powered educational platform.

Do not approach this project as a conventional LMS.

Do not approach it as a chatbot with courses around it.

Do not approach it as a static course generator.

Do not approach it as a collection of autonomous agents.

Build an actual **Learning Intelligence Platform**.

The central purpose is to understand the learner's current state, understand the learning domain, construct or interpret an appropriate curriculum, determine what the learner should do next, evaluate the resulting evidence, adapt the learning path, and progressively increase the learner's independent competence.

The platform must work both when a learner has educational materials and when the learner provides only a goal.

The application must remain independent of any specific AI provider, model, API key, course provider, or individual content source.

The ultimate objective is **real learning, not superficial activity**.

---

# PART I — PRODUCT VISION AND EDUCATIONAL MODEL

## 1. Product Vision

Create an intelligent educational environment in which the system continuously develops an evidence-based understanding of each learner.

The system should answer:

- What does this learner know?
- What does this learner probably not know?
- Which concepts are only superficially understood?
- Which misconceptions are present?
- Which skills have been forgotten?
- Which skills are stable?
- What can the learner solve independently?
- What can the learner solve only with help?
- What can the learner transfer to new situations?
- Which strategies does the learner use?
- What currently blocks progress?
- What is the highest-value next learning action?
- Why was that action selected?
- What evidence supports this decision?
- Did the intervention work?

## 2. North Star

The platform succeeds when the learner becomes:

- more knowledgeable;
- more capable;
- more independent;
- better at reasoning;
- better at transferring knowledge;
- better at detecting mistakes;
- better at learning.

The product should deliberately reduce the learner's dependence on AI as their competence increases.

Do not optimize primarily for engagement, session duration, AI usage, or lesson completion.

## 3. What the Product Is

The product is an adaptive learning intelligence system built conceptually around:

**Student Learning Twin + Knowledge Graph + Evidence Engine + Curriculum Intelligence + Content Intelligence + Assessment Intelligence + Learning Strategist + AI Orchestration.**

The AI layer is an enabling component.

The educational model is the foundation.

The learner's durable learning state is the source of truth.

## 4. What the Product Must Not Become

Do not build:

- a standard LMS;
- a static course website;
- a chatbot with uploaded PDFs;
- a generic question-answering assistant;
- an AI-only grader;
- a random exercise generator;
- a fixed roadmap for everyone;
- a one-model application;
- a one-provider application;
- a system that requires uploaded content before it can teach;
- an opaque recommendation engine;
- a system where every decision requires an AI call;
- a system where every problem is solved by an agent;
- a gamification engine disguised as education.

## 5. Primary Domains

The initial domains are:

- Machine Learning;
- Programming;
- Algorithms;
- Data Structures;
- Competitive Programming;
- Mathematics;
- Statistics.

The architecture must support new domains without requiring a complete redesign.

## 6. Domain-Agnostic Design

Separate educational intelligence from domain content.

Domain-independent systems include:

- learner modeling;
- assessment;
- mastery;
- evidence;
- curriculum planning;
- recommendations;
- tutoring;
- retention;
- transfer;
- intervention;
- analytics.

Domain-specific systems include:

- concepts;
- skills;
- prerequisites;
- misconceptions;
- task types;
- examples;
- curriculum content;
- domain-specific verification.

A new domain should primarily require new domain knowledge and domain-specific evaluators, not a new application architecture.

## 7. Educational Foundation

Use a coherent synthesis of:

- Zone of Proximal Development;
- fading of support;
- mastery learning;
- cognitive load adaptation;
- worked examples;
- completion problems;
- retrieval practice;
- spaced practice;
- interleaving;
- productive failure;
- formative assessment;
- metacognition;
- self-regulated learning;
- transfer;
- complex whole-task learning;
- misconception-driven intervention;
- adaptive assessment.

Do not apply these principles mechanically.

Resolve conflicts using the learner's state.

For example:

- beginners may need worked examples;
- sufficiently prepared learners may benefit from productive failure;
- mastery gates may control progression;
- interleaving may maintain mastered skills without blocking progression;
- support should decrease with expertise;
- discovery should be guided rather than blindly imposed;
- autonomy should increase with competence.

## 8. Core Learning Loop

The main learning loop is:

**Goal → Diagnosis → Competency State → Roadmap → Learning Action → Attempt → Verification → Evidence → Student Learning Twin Update → Intervention → New Attempt → Independent Performance → Mastery → Retention → Transfer → Next Competency.**

Every major subsystem should support this loop.

## 9. Assessment Is Part of Learning

Assessment is not merely a score-generation mechanism.

Assessment must influence what happens next.

A useful assessment should determine something meaningful, such as whether the learner:

- has mastered a skill;
- only recognizes a familiar pattern;
- depends on assistance;
- has a misconception;
- has forgotten a previously learned concept;
- can transfer a skill;
- can explain a concept;
- can solve the problem under changed conditions.

---

# PART II — STUDENT LEARNING TWIN AND EVIDENCE

## 10. Student Learning Twin

Create a persistent model called the **Student Learning Twin**.

This is an educational state model based on observed evidence.

It is not a psychological diagnosis or a hidden personality profile.

It should represent:

- goals;
- competencies;
- mastery;
- uncertainty;
- depth of understanding;
- independence;
- application;
- transfer;
- retention;
- misconceptions;
- process quality;
- strategy quality;
- metacognition;
- confidence calibration;
- help-seeking;
- recent performance;
- historical evidence;
- learning trajectory.

## 11. Competency Vector

For each meaningful skill, maintain at least these dimensions:

1. Mastery.
2. Cognitive Depth.
3. Independence.
4. Application / Integration.
5. Transfer.
6. Retention.
7. Misconceptions.
8. Process / Strategy.

Do not reduce the learner to one global score.

A student may know a concept but still be unable to apply it independently or transfer it to new tasks.

## 12. Mastery

Mastery represents the estimated probability or state that a learner can use a skill successfully under relevant conditions.

Do not treat reading, watching, claiming familiarity, or answering one easy question as sufficient proof of mastery.

## 13. Mastery Uncertainty

Represent uncertainty explicitly.

Examples:

- strongly supported;
- moderately supported;
- uncertain;
- insufficient evidence.

Do not create fake precision simply because the UI can display percentages.

## 14. Cognitive Depth

Track how deeply a concept has been demonstrated.

Possible levels include:

- recognition;
- understanding;
- application;
- analysis;
- comparison;
- justification;
- creation;
- transfer.

A learner should not receive strong mastery confidence solely because they can recognize a familiar definition.

## 15. Independence

Track whether the learner can perform without assistance.

Evidence may include:

- hints;
- hint level;
- support provided;
- retries;
- tutor interventions;
- independent success;
- recovery behavior.

Distinguish independent mastery from supported competence.

## 16. Application and Integration

Measure the ability to combine multiple skills.

A student may know recursion, trees, and graphs individually while struggling when a task requires all three.

Integrated tasks should provide evidence about this dimension.

## 17. Transfer

Measure whether the learner can use knowledge in new contexts.

Transfer tasks should change meaningful aspects of a problem, such as:

- context;
- representation;
- constraints;
- combination of skills;
- wording;
- data;
- assumptions.

Track transfer separately from repetition.

## 18. Retention

Track whether previously learned information remains accessible over time.

Include:

- successful retrieval;
- time since last successful retrieval;
- repeated failures;
- recovery after cues;
- long-term stability.

## 19. Misconceptions

Maintain explicit misconception states associated with skills.

Each misconception should have:

- identifier;
- description;
- affected skills;
- supporting evidence;
- confidence;
- triggers;
- corrective explanations;
- corrective tasks;
- resolution criteria.

## 20. Process and Strategy

Track observable aspects of the learner's process, such as:

- planning;
- hypothesis formation;
- validation;
- debugging;
- strategy selection;
- error detection;
- revision;
- self-checking.

Do not attempt to reconstruct hidden chain-of-thought. Use explicit learner-authored artifacts and observable interaction traces.

## 21. Metacognitive Profile

Track observable:

- confidence;
- calibration;
- planning;
- monitoring;
- reflection;
- help-seeking;
- strategy awareness.

Use this information to teach better self-regulation.

## 22. Evidence Engine

Create a dedicated Evidence Engine.

Its responsibility is to collect, structure, weight, and connect evidence to learning-state updates.

Potential evidence includes:

- task results;
- test results;
- code execution;
- explanations;
- solution artifacts;
- hints;
- retries;
- confidence;
- transfer tasks;
- retention checks;
- projects;
- teacher feedback;
- reflections;
- observable interaction traces.

## 23. Evidence Lineage

For significant learner-model updates, preserve provenance.

Record where appropriate:

- event;
- learner;
- skill;
- task;
- attempt;
- support used;
- verification method;
- result;
- timestamp;
- resulting state update.

The system should be able to explain why a particular competency state exists.

## 24. Evidence Strength

Evidence should have different reliability levels.

Stronger evidence may include:

- deterministic tests;
- repeated independent success;
- verified project outcomes;
- transfer tasks.

Supporting evidence may include:

- AI interpretation;
- explanation quality;
- confidence;
- behavioral traces.

Do not treat all forms of evidence as equivalent.

## 25. AI Is Not Ground Truth

AI-generated evaluation is not automatically authoritative.

When objective verification exists, use it.

For programming:

**Execution + Tests + Static Analysis + AI Interpretation**

For mathematics:

**Verification + Rubric + Learner Reasoning + AI Interpretation**

For conceptual learning:

**Rubric + Explanation + Transfer + Repeated Evidence**

AI assists the evaluation system; it does not replace objective verification.

---

# PART III — KNOWLEDGE GRAPH AND CURRICULUM

## 26. Knowledge Graph

Create a structured knowledge graph containing:

- domains;
- concepts;
- skills;
- subskills;
- prerequisites;
- dependencies;
- conceptual relationships;
- misconceptions;
- task types;
- content;
- assessments;
- transfer contexts.

## 27. Prerequisite Graph

Represent prerequisite relationships as structured data.

Example:

`Algebra → Functions → Derivatives → Gradients → Optimization → Machine Learning`

Do not assume every relationship is perfectly deterministic.

Treat expert knowledge as a useful prior that can be refined using learning evidence.

## 28. Graph Versioning

Knowledge graph changes must be versioned.

Track changes to:

- prerequisites;
- concepts;
- relationships;
- difficulty;
- skill definitions.

Do not silently change the meaning of an existing skill.

## 29. Curriculum Intelligence

Transform:

**Goal + Domain + Learner State + Competency Graph**

into:

**Personalized Learning Path**.

The curriculum system should determine:

- what to learn;
- what can be skipped;
- what needs repair;
- what should be delayed;
- when to introduce advanced concepts;
- when to test transfer;
- when to schedule review;
- when to introduce projects.

## 30. Dynamic Curriculum

The curriculum is a plan, not a permanent contract.

Examples:

`diagnostic → prerequisite mastered → skip introductory block`

`task failures → missing prerequisite detected → prerequisite repair inserted`

`strong independent performance → accelerate → increase challenge`

`persistent misconception → targeted intervention inserted`

## 31. Goal Compiler

Accept natural-language learning goals.

Examples:

- “I want to learn Python from zero.”
- “I know Python but want competitive programming.”
- “I want to become an ML engineer.”
- “I need to prepare for an algorithms interview.”
- “I want to master statistics.”

Convert a goal into:

**Desired Outcome → Competencies → Prerequisites → Roadmap → Milestones → Assessments → Projects.**

## 32. Multiple Goals

A learner may have multiple goals, such as passing an exam, learning ML, preparing for interviews, and improving competitive programming.

Recognize shared prerequisites and avoid duplicate work.

## 33. Goal Changes

When the learner changes goals:

- preserve historical learning evidence;
- preserve competencies;
- recalculate target competencies;
- generate a new roadmap;
- reuse relevant mastered knowledge.

Never restart the learner from zero.

---

# PART IV — COLD START, CONTENT INGESTION AND ENRICHMENT

## 34. Cold Start

The platform must work when **zero educational materials are uploaded**.

An empty material library is a valid state, not an error.

The application must be able to start from a goal alone.

## 35. Autonomous Roadmap Generation

When no materials are supplied, generate an initial roadmap from:

- learning goal;
- domain structure;
- prerequisite relationships;
- desired level;
- learner diagnosis;
- target outcome;
- available study time.

Where appropriate, include:

- phases;
- topics;
- skills;
- prerequisites;
- lessons;
- examples;
- tasks;
- assessments;
- projects;
- transfer checkpoints;
- retention checkpoints.

## 36. Roadmap as a Hypothesis

A generated roadmap must adapt as evidence arrives.

Do not force the learner through every generated lesson.

## 37. Roadmap Preview

Show a useful high-level preview:

- destination;
- major stages;
- expected competencies;
- projects;
- assessments;
- prerequisites;
- approximate sequence.

Communicate that the roadmap can adapt as the system learns more about the student.

## 38. Roadmap Personalization

The same domain should produce different paths for different learners.

A beginner may need foundations, worked examples, guided practice, independent practice, and transfer.

An advanced learner may need diagnostics, selected gap repair, advanced problems, projects, and transfer.

## 39. Curriculum and Content Are Different

Separate:

**Curriculum:** what should be learned.

**Content:** how the learner is taught.

A valid curriculum can exist before all its content has been created.

## 40. Content Sources

Content may come from:

- user uploads;
- teacher uploads;
- institution materials;
- approved external sources;
- generated explanations;
- generated examples;
- generated exercises;
- generated projects.

## 41. Content Provenance

Every educational content item must record its origin.

Possible types:

- user-provided;
- teacher-provided;
- institution-provided;
- external;
- generated;
- generated-from-source;
- enriched;
- adapted.

Do not blur these categories.

## 42. Canonical Content

Canonical content is explicitly trusted or approved content.

The system must not silently overwrite it.

## 43. Generated Content

Represent generated content as a separate layer.

Possible states:

- draft;
- generated;
- validated;
- approved;
- deprecated.

## 44. Material Ingestion

When a user uploads a book or document, analyze it rather than merely adding it to a chatbot context.

Extract where possible:

- concepts;
- skills;
- definitions;
- examples;
- prerequisites;
- exercises;
- projects;
- misconceptions;
- conceptual distinctions;
- learning objectives;
- assessment opportunities;
- difficulty.

## 45. Content Coverage Map

Compare available materials with the target curriculum.

Determine:

- covered competencies;
- partially covered competencies;
- missing competencies;
- missing prerequisites;
- missing examples;
- missing practice;
- missing assessments;
- missing transfer tasks.

## 46. Gap Detection

When materials are incomplete, identify what is missing.

Do not assume that a large collection of documents is sufficient simply because it contains many pages.

## 47. Material Enrichment Toggle

Provide a clear setting:

**Allow automatic enrichment of learning materials**

This determines whether the system may persistently add educational content that was not part of the original materials.

## 48. Enrichment OFF

When enrichment is OFF, the system may:

- explain concepts;
- provide temporary explanations;
- create temporary exercises;
- identify curriculum gaps;
- suggest external resources where supported;
- suggest missing topics.

However, it must not silently add generated content to the persistent educational library.

## 49. Enrichment ON

When enrichment is ON, the system may:

- create missing lessons;
- create examples;
- create exercises;
- create quizzes;
- create project tasks;
- create transfer tasks;
- create glossary entries;
- create prerequisite material;
- incorporate additional approved material.

All additions must preserve provenance.

## 50. Enrichment Approval Modes

Support configurable policies:

**Manual Approval:** every persistent addition requires approval.

**Automatic:** content can become persistent according to configured rules.

**Suggestion Only:** the system proposes additions but does not persist them.

## 51. Never Overwrite Original Material

Never silently replace user-provided or teacher-approved material.

Conceptually separate:

`Original Material + Generated Supplement + External Material + Personalized Adaptation`.

## 52. Temporary Versus Persistent Content

Not every AI response should become part of a course.

Temporary output is useful for the current interaction.

Persistent educational assets are intentionally stored as learning content.

Persistence must follow the enrichment policy.

## 53. Generated Content Validation

Before important generated material becomes persistent, validate where practical for:

- correctness;
- relevance;
- curriculum alignment;
- prerequisites;
- difficulty;
- duplication;
- contradictions;
- quality;
- assessment suitability.

## 54. High-Stakes Content

For high-stakes education, use stronger validation, allow teacher approval, preserve provenance, and avoid presenting uncertain content as authoritative.

## 55. Conflicting Materials

If generated content conflicts with canonical content:

- detect the conflict;
- preserve both sources;
- identify the disagreement;
- use configured source-priority rules;
- optionally request human review.

Do not silently merge contradictions.

## 56. Content Quality Loop

Use:

**Generate → Validate → Use → Observe Learning Outcome → Improve → Version.**

## 57. Content Retirement

Support:

- disabling bad content;
- marking obsolete content;
- replacing generated material;
- versioning;
- preserving history.

## 58. Content Deduplication

Before generating content, search for existing relevant material.

Reuse or adapt it when appropriate.

Generate new content only when it adds educational value.

## 59. Content Personalization

Generated explanations and examples should consider:

- learner level;
- current skill;
- misconception;
- previous attempts;
- current goal;
- roadmap position;
- prerequisites.

Avoid repeating generic explanations unnecessarily.

## 60. Multiple Representations

Use appropriate representations:

- text;
- formulas;
- code;
- diagrams;
- examples;
- counterexamples;
- visualizations;
- simulations.

Use them when they improve understanding, not as decoration.

## 61. Content Graph

Connect content to competencies.

For example:

`Lesson A → Skill X`

`Task B → Skill X + Skill Y`

`Project C → Skills X + Y + Z`

## 62. Empty Course Experience

An empty learning environment must be a valid onboarding state.

The learner should be able to specify:

- subject;
- goal;
- desired level;
- deadline;
- available time;
- desired outcome.

The platform should generate a starting point.

## 63. Later Material Upload

If the learner starts without materials and uploads them later:

1. analyze the material;
2. identify covered competencies;
3. compare with the current roadmap;
4. identify gaps;
5. integrate relevant material;
6. preserve learner state.

Never restart the curriculum.

## 64. Course Templates

Reusable domain templates may exist, but they must remain adaptable.

Use:

**General Curriculum Template + Personalized Learner Adaptation.**

## 65. External Information and Grounding

If external retrieval is supported:

- preserve source provenance;
- distinguish external information from canonical material;
- do not fabricate citations;
- preserve retrieval context where appropriate.

Distinguish stable educational knowledge from time-sensitive information.

---

# PART V — DIAGNOSIS, MASTERY AND ADAPTATION

## 66. Diagnostic Assessment

Determine:

- starting level;
- prerequisites;
- mastery;
- uncertainty;
- misconceptions;
- independence.

Use adaptive diagnosis where appropriate.

## 67. Active Assessment

Select questions and tasks that provide meaningful information.

Do not collect data simply because it is available.

## 68. Mastery Estimation

Use probabilistic or otherwise uncertainty-aware mastery estimation.

Mastery should change in response to evidence.

## 69. Mastery Gates

Use mastery gates at meaningful progression points.

Evidence may include:

- standard task;
- independent task;
- changed task;
- transfer task;
- retention check.

Avoid hundreds of rigid gates.

## 70. Adaptive Instruction Format

Adapt according to the learner's level:

- low mastery → worked example;
- developing mastery → completion problem;
- moderate mastery → guided task;
- high mastery → independent task;
- stable high mastery → transfer or complex task.

## 71. Expertise Reversal

When the learner becomes strong enough, previously useful support may become unnecessary or counterproductive.

Detect when scaffolding can be reduced.

## 72. Hint Ladder

Use progressive support:

1. attention cue;
2. guiding question;
3. concept reminder;
4. strategy hint;
5. next-step hint;
6. partial solution;
7. complete solution.

Choose the smallest intervention likely to help.

## 73. Hint Dependency

Track whether the learner increasingly depends on hints.

High hint dependence should trigger lower-support tasks, delayed help, reflection, and independent checkpoints.

## 74. Fading

Use a progression such as:

`Worked Example → Completion → Guided → Independent → Transfer`.

## 75. Productive Failure

For suitable learners and concepts:

`Attempt → Exploration → Partial Solutions → Analysis → Consolidation → Transfer`.

Do not force productive failure on learners lacking prerequisites.

## 76. Cognitive Load

For beginners, provide structure, examples, and reduced irrelevant complexity.

For experts, reduce redundancy and scaffolding while increasing novelty and independent planning.

## 77. Desirable Difficulty

Use productive difficulty through retrieval, delayed review, interleaving, novelty, reduced support, and transfer.

Do not create arbitrary frustration.

## 78. Personal Difficulty

Estimate difficulty using:

- learner mastery;
- prerequisites;
- recent performance;
- independence;
- novelty;
- task family;
- cognitive load;
- time.

## 79. Task Intelligence

Each meaningful task should have metadata for:

- target skill;
- supporting skills;
- prerequisites;
- difficulty;
- cognitive level;
- task type;
- expected duration;
- misconception coverage;
- transfer level;
- independence requirement.

## 80. Task Families

Represent educationally equivalent tasks.

Examples:

- same algorithm, different data;
- same concept, different context;
- same misconception, different distractor;
- same programming skill, different constraints.

## 81. Task Mutation

Meaningfully vary:

- context;
- numbers;
- data;
- constraints;
- representation;
- required output;
- combination of skills.

Do not create cosmetic variations that test memorization.

## 82. Solution Diversity

Support multiple valid solutions.

Distinguish:

- incorrect;
- correct but inefficient;
- correct and reasonable;
- excellent;
- correct but outside required constraints.

---

# PART VI — MISCONCEPTIONS, RETENTION AND TRANSFER

## 83. Misconception Engine

Maintain explicit misconception states linked to:

- skill;
- evidence;
- confidence;
- likely cause;
- corrective intervention;
- verification.

## 84. Error Fingerprints

Detect recurring patterns such as:

- off-by-one;
- wrong base case;
- incorrect complexity;
- algorithm misselection;
- misuse of formulas;
- incorrect probability assumptions;
- correlation/causation confusion;
- overfitting;
- wrong boundary condition.

## 85. Misconception Intervention

Use:

**Detect → Explain → Challenge → Retry → Verify.**

Do not mark a misconception as resolved after one correct answer.

## 86. Forgetting Versus Misunderstanding

When performance drops, distinguish:

- never mastered;
- mastered before but currently forgotten;
- misconception;
- strategy failure;
- execution or attention mistake.

Different causes require different interventions.

## 87. Retention Engine

Schedule review based on:

- prior success;
- elapsed time;
- difficulty;
- importance;
- confidence;
- forgetting risk.

## 88. Spaced Retrieval

Use active retrieval instead of relying primarily on rereading, passive review, or repeated explanations.

## 89. Interleaving

Mix skills strategically so the learner practices selecting methods rather than repeating one recent pattern.

## 90. Review Mix

A session may include:

- new learning;
- due review;
- weak-skill practice;
- transfer;
- reflection.

Balance review against progress.

## 91. Transfer Engine

Explicitly schedule transfer tasks.

A transfer task must differ meaningfully from the training example.

## 92. Concept Contrast

Use comparisons such as:

- BFS vs DFS;
- bias vs variance;
- precision vs recall;
- supervised vs unsupervised learning;
- correlation vs causation.

## 93. Boundary Tasks

Ask:

- When does this method work?
- When does it fail?
- What assumptions are required?
- What changes when an assumption is removed?

## 94. Counterexample Engine

Use counterexamples to challenge overgeneralization.

## 95. Feynman / Teach-Back

Ask learners to explain concepts in their own words.

Evaluate correctness, conceptual relationships, missing assumptions, contradictions, and completeness.

Do not equate vocabulary sophistication with understanding.

## 96. Socratic Tutor

The tutor should sometimes ask questions rather than answer immediately.

Possible actions:

- explain;
- ask a question;
- provide a hint;
- request an example;
- request a counterexample;
- ask the learner to test a hypothesis.

## 97. Oral Examination Mode

Support adaptive oral examination. The examiner can ask, challenge, change conditions, request justification, challenge assumptions, and follow up on weak answers.

## 98. AI Peer Mode

Allow learners to inspect an AI-generated student answer and identify mistakes.

Measure error detection, conceptual reasoning, explanation, and confidence.

## 99. Metacognition

Explicitly teach learners to plan, monitor, reflect, calibrate confidence, and seek help appropriately.

## 100. Confidence Calibration

Before selected tasks, ask how confident the learner is.

Compare predicted confidence with actual outcomes and use the results for metacognitive training.

---

# PART VII — AI INTERACTION ARCHITECTURE

## 101. AI Interaction Is Not Generic Chat

Do not implement AI as a generic chat interface that receives a message and returns an isolated response.

AI interaction must be connected to the learner's current educational state.

The conceptual flow is:

**User Input → Intent Detection → Context Builder → Student Learning Twin → Knowledge Graph → Current Objective → AI Orchestrator → Specialized Agent → Provider Adapter → Structured Output → Verification → Evidence → Learning State Update → Next Action.**

## 102. Intent Detection

Classify meaningful learner interactions into educational intents, such as:

- explanation;
- clarification;
- task assistance;
- debugging;
- code review;
- practice request;
- task generation;
- assessment;
- exam mode;
- planning;
- roadmap request;
- reflection;
- misconception investigation;
- project assistance;
- transfer practice;
- retention review.

Do not use identical behavior for every intent.

## 103. Context Builder

Before calling an AI model, construct a task-specific context.

Possible context includes:

- current goal;
- current roadmap position;
- current skill;
- relevant prerequisites;
- competency state;
- mastery estimate;
- independence;
- relevant misconceptions;
- recent attempts;
- recent errors;
- hint history;
- retention status;
- transfer status;
- current task;
- relevant educational content;
- current conversation context.

Do not automatically send the entire learner history.

## 104. Context Hierarchy

Prefer context in roughly this order:

1. current task;
2. current learning objective;
3. relevant skill;
4. relevant prerequisites;
5. recent evidence;
6. relevant learner state;
7. relevant educational content;
8. broader conversation context.

Do not include unrelated learner history just because it is available.

## 105. Contextual Chat

The AI tutor should understand the current educational situation.

If the learner asks, “Why does this return None?”, the system should be able to understand the current Python task, submitted code, latest test failure, relevant concept, and recent error pattern.

The learner should not have to repeatedly reconstruct context.

## 106. Tutor Intervention Selection

When a learner asks for help, choose the smallest useful intervention.

Possible responses:

- clarifying question;
- conceptual hint;
- strategy hint;
- example;
- counterexample;
- partial explanation;
- complete explanation.

Consider current mastery, independence, previous hints, task difficulty, misconceptions, and assessment mode.

Do not automatically reveal the answer.

## 107. Conversation as Micro-Assessment

A conversation may produce learning evidence.

Example:

`Tutor explains → asks a follow-up question → learner answers → answer is evaluated → evidence is created → learner state is updated.`

Do not count every chat message as evidence. Only meaningful, interpretable interactions should affect the learner model.

## 108. Conversation-to-Intervention

If a conversation reveals a possible misconception, transition to a diagnostic question or targeted task when appropriate.

Do not permanently mark a misconception based on one ambiguous conversation.

## 109. AI Interaction Modes

Support distinct modes:

- Explain;
- Hint;
- Socratic;
- Debug;
- Code Review;
- Independent Practice;
- Assessment;
- Oral Examination;
- Reflection;
- Project Mentor.

Each mode must define its behavior and assistance policy.

## 110. AI Action Types

Use explicit action types rather than relying only on free-form text.

Examples:

- `ASK_QUESTION`;
- `GIVE_HINT`;
- `EXPLAIN`;
- `SHOW_EXAMPLE`;
- `GIVE_COUNTEREXAMPLE`;
- `REQUEST_ATTEMPT`;
- `REQUEST_REFLECTION`;
- `CREATE_DIAGNOSTIC`;
- `ESCALATE`.

## 111. Context Window Discipline

Do not pass the entire learner history, course, knowledge graph, event log, and conversation into every AI call.

Use context retrieval and task-specific context construction.

## 112. Chat Memory

Conversation history may support the current session.

Long-term educational memory must reside in the Student Learning Twin and Evidence Engine.

Do not use raw chat logs as a substitute for learner modeling.

## 113. AI Session State

A learning session may contain:

- current task;
- learner state;
- conversation context;
- selected tools;
- active objective;
- allowed support level.

Only meaningful evidence should persist after the interaction.

---

# PART VIII — TASK GENERATION ARCHITECTURE

## 114. Task Generation Is Not a Random Prompt

Do not implement task generation as:

`"Generate a Python task" → LLM → Task`.

Instead use:

**Learner State → Learning Objective → Skill Selection → Task Specification → Task Family → Difficulty → Cognitive Level → Misconception Target → Independence Requirement → Transfer Requirement → Existing Task Search → Reuse / Mutate / Generate → Validation → Approval → Student Attempt.**

## 115. Learning Objective First

Before generating a task, define its educational purpose.

Examples:

- practice loops;
- diagnose misunderstanding of recursion;
- verify independent use of functions;
- test transfer of sorting concepts;
- strengthen retrieval of probability rules.

Never generate a task without a defined educational objective.

## 116. Skill Selection

Select target skills from the Knowledge Graph and Student Learning Twin.

The task system should know:

- target skill;
- supporting skills;
- prerequisite skills;
- mastery;
- independence;
- active misconceptions;
- previous tasks;
- evidence history.

## 117. Task Specification

Create an internal task specification before generating the actual task.

Example:

```json
{
  "target_skill": "python.functions.return_values",
  "supporting_skills": [
    "python.functions",
    "python.control_flow"
  ],
  "task_family": "function-output",
  "difficulty": 0.58,
  "cognitive_level": "apply",
  "misconception_targets": [
    "missing-return"
  ],
  "independence_required": 0.8,
  "transfer_level": 0.2,
  "expected_time_minutes": 10
}
```

The exact schema may differ, but preserve the separation between what the task must teach or test and how it is worded.

## 118. Task Family

Tasks should belong to task families representing an underlying educational objective.

Examples:

- recursion base cases;
- function return values;
- binary search boundary handling;
- precision vs recall;
- gradient descent interpretation.

Multiple tasks may share a task family while differing in data, context, wording, constraints, and representation.

## 119. Task Difficulty

Personalize difficulty based on the learner's state.

Consider:

- complexity;
- novelty;
- amount of structure;
- available support;
- integrated skills;
- constraints;
- prior performance.

Do not rely only on a static global difficulty value.

## 120. Cognitive Level

Specify the intended cognitive demand of each important task.

Possible levels:

- recognition;
- understanding;
- application;
- analysis;
- evaluation;
- creation;
- transfer.

## 121. Misconception-Targeted Tasks

When a misconception is active, generate tasks that can reveal or correct it.

The task must make the misconception observable.

## 122. Independence Requirement

The task specification should state whether support is:

- unrestricted;
- limited;
- unavailable.

This affects both the task experience and interpretation of its evidence.

## 123. Transfer Requirement

Classify a task as:

- repetition;
- variation;
- integration;
- transfer;
- open-ended.

Transfer tasks must differ meaningfully from training examples.

## 124. Task Generation

AI may generate:

- task statement;
- examples;
- starter code;
- input/output format;
- distractors;
- hints;
- expected reasoning criteria;
- reference solution;
- test cases.

Generated output must remain subject to validation.

## 125. Task Validation

Before presenting a generated task, validate where practical for:

- schema correctness;
- prerequisite consistency;
- skill alignment;
- difficulty;
- duplication;
- misconception alignment;
- solution correctness;
- test execution;
- edge cases.

## 126. Programming Task Validation

For programming tasks, use a stronger pipeline:

`Task Specification → Task Generation → Reference Solution → Test Generation → Reference Execution → Edge-Case Validation → Task Approval`.

Do not publish a task if the reference solution fails its own tests or the task is internally inconsistent.

## 127. Mathematics and Statistics Validation

Where possible, use:

- symbolic verification;
- numerical verification;
- executable checks;
- answer constraints;
- rubric validation.

Do not trust mathematical output solely because it sounds plausible.

## 128. Task Quality Control

Detect:

- ambiguous wording;
- missing information;
- impossible conditions;
- unintended interpretations;
- incorrect expected answers;
- inappropriate difficulty;
- irrelevant concepts;
- accidental answer leakage.

## 129. Task Deduplication

Before generating a new task:

1. search existing task families;
2. identify similar tasks;
3. determine whether variation is useful;
4. generate only if it adds educational value.

## 130. Task Equivalence

Track equivalence through:

- task family;
- target skills;
- misconception coverage;
- cognitive level;
- difficulty;
- transfer level.

Use this information when estimating mastery.

## 131. Task Mutation

Generate meaningful variations by changing:

- context;
- numbers;
- data;
- constraints;
- representation;
- combinations of skills.

Do not merely change names or superficial wording.

## 132. Generated Hints

Generate hints from the task specification and learner state.

Follow the hint ladder. Do not give every learner the same hint strength.

## 133. Generated Explanations

Explanations should consider:

- learner level;
- prior knowledge;
- misconception;
- current task;
- previous explanations;
- learning goal.

## 134. Task Selection Priority

Prefer a validated existing task when it satisfies the learning objective.

Otherwise, mutate an existing task.

Generate an entirely new task when it adds meaningful educational value.

---

# PART IX — PROGRAMMING, PROJECTS AND PRACTICAL LEARNING

## 135. Programming Workspace

Provide a professional coding environment that supports:

- writing code;
- execution;
- tests;
- error output;
- attempts;
- debugging;
- submission;
- code review;
- hints.

## 136. Code Execution Security

Student code must execute in isolation.

Use:

- sandboxing;
- process isolation;
- CPU limits;
- memory limits;
- time limits;
- filesystem restrictions;
- network restrictions.

Never execute untrusted code directly inside the primary application process.

## 137. Code Intelligence

Combine where possible:

- deterministic tests;
- execution;
- static analysis;
- AST information;
- complexity analysis;
- code structure;
- AI interpretation.

## 138. AI Code Review

Prioritize:

1. correctness;
2. bugs;
3. algorithmic logic;
4. complexity;
5. structure;
6. maintainability;
7. style.

## 139. Code Review as Teaching

A review should explain:

- what happened;
- why it is a problem;
- which concept is involved;
- how the learner can discover the solution.

Do not merely output rewritten code.

## 140. Line-Level Feedback

Where possible, attach comments to exact lines, code ranges, or relevant expressions.

## 141. Debugging Intelligence

Treat debugging as a competency.

Observe:

- first hypothesis;
- experiment;
- code change;
- test;
- outcome;
- next hypothesis;
- strategy switch.

## 142. Debugging Feedback

Teach systematic debugging.

Prefer guiding the learner to discover the problem over immediately revealing the answer.

## 143. Competitive Programming

Support:

- timed tasks;
- algorithm selection;
- complexity;
- edge cases;
- debugging;
- contest simulation;
- post-contest analysis.

Separate competition performance from general conceptual mastery.

## 144. Machine Learning Path

Support progression through:

- programming;
- mathematics;
- statistics;
- data handling;
- ML fundamentals;
- model evaluation;
- experimentation;
- error analysis;
- projects.

## 145. ML Failure Learning

Use realistic failure cases:

- overfitting;
- data leakage;
- class imbalance;
- distribution shift;
- poor metrics;
- validation mistakes;
- corrupted data;
- misleading results.

## 146. Real-World Projects

Progress from:

`Toy → Structured → Realistic → Open-Ended → Project`.

## 147. Project Intelligence

Track:

- objective;
- plan;
- requirements;
- milestones;
- decisions;
- implementation;
- failed approaches;
- revisions;
- final output;
- reflection.

## 148. Project Assessment

Evaluate both the final product and the learner's process.

## 149. Project Mentor

The project mentor should guide without taking ownership of the project.

It should ask questions, identify risks, support planning, provide hints, review decisions, and request validation.

## 150. Assignment Copilot

Allow external assignments to enter the platform.

Identify relevant competencies, prerequisites, preparation steps, and learning objectives.

Do not automatically complete academic work for the learner.

## 151. Notebook

Support notes, reflections, solution summaries, questions, mistakes, and observations.

## 152. Personal Glossary

Maintain learner-specific concept explanations linked to skills, examples, misconceptions, review, and notes.

## 153. Knowledge Inbox

Allow learners to save links, questions, screenshots, ideas, confusing concepts, and external material.

Help convert these into structured learning opportunities.

---

# PART X — AGENTIC LEARNING ARCHITECTURE

## 154. Agentic Architecture

Use agents where they genuinely improve the educational product.

Do not create agents merely for the sake of using agents.

## 155. Agent Orchestrator

Create a centralized orchestration layer.

Potential agents:

- Learning Strategist;
- Tutor;
- Diagnostic Agent;
- Assessment Agent;
- Curriculum Agent;
- Content Agent;
- Misconception Agent;
- Retention Agent;
- Code Review Agent;
- Debugging Agent;
- Project Mentor;
- Examiner;
- Research Agent;
- Verification Agent;
- Critic Agent.

## 156. Agent Responsibilities

Each agent must have:

- explicit purpose;
- allowed inputs;
- allowed tools;
- output schema;
- permissions;
- failure behavior;
- verification process;
- evaluation criteria.

## 157. Shared Agent State

All agents must use shared educational state:

**Student Learning Twin + Knowledge Graph + Evidence Engine.**

Do not create isolated, contradictory learner models.

## 158. Agent Memory

Separate:

- long-term educational memory;
- current task memory;
- temporary agent working state.

Raw chat history must not be the primary educational database.

## 159. Agent Permissions

Examples:

- Tutor: explain, hint, ask questions.
- Assessment Agent: evaluate and submit assessment evidence.
- Curriculum Agent: propose roadmap changes.
- Content Agent: generate material.
- Verification Agent: validate.
- Teacher: approve or override.

No agent should silently modify critical state beyond its permissions.

## 160. Agent Pipeline

Use:

**Agent → Tool → Verification → Evidence → State Update.**

## 161. Agent State Machine

Important agents should follow:

**Observe → Analyze → Act → Verify → Update.**

Avoid uncontrolled autonomous loops.

## 162. Agent Limits

Use:

- maximum retries;
- maximum tool calls;
- timeouts;
- escalation rules;
- confidence thresholds.

## 163. Agent Failure

Support retry, fallback, verification, uncertainty, escalation, and human review.

## 164. Agent Council

Use multiple agents only when the task benefits from multiple perspectives.

Do not call every agent for every interaction.

## 165. Python Agent Stack

Use Python for the intelligence and agent layer.

Default technologies:

- Python;
- FastAPI;
- Pydantic;
- PydanticAI;
- LangGraph.

## 166. PydanticAI Role

Use PydanticAI where typed agent contracts and structured outputs are useful.

Avoid unstructured agent-to-agent text as the primary integration mechanism.

## 167. LangGraph Role

Use LangGraph for stateful agent orchestration where appropriate, including:

- agent state;
- branching workflows;
- tool interactions;
- multi-step reasoning;
- agent-level human-in-the-loop;
- controlled execution.

Do not use LangGraph as a substitute for the entire product architecture.

## 168. Temporal Role

Use Temporal for durable long-running workflows, including:

- content ingestion;
- large-document analysis;
- curriculum generation;
- content enrichment;
- approval workflows;
- long-running assessment;
- asynchronous processing;
- durable recovery.

## 169. LangGraph Versus Temporal

They are not interchangeable.

**LangGraph:** agent orchestration and agent state.

**Temporal:** durable business workflows and long-running processes.

Use each where appropriate.

## 170. AI Dependency Reduction

Reduce support as learner competence grows.

As competence increases:

- explanations become shorter;
- hints weaken;
- independent attempts increase;
- transfer increases;
- self-checking increases.

---

# PART XI — TECHNOLOGY STACK AND ARCHITECTURE

## 171. Default Technology Stack

Use the following as the default technical foundation.

**Frontend**
- Next.js;
- React;
- TypeScript;
- Tailwind CSS;
- shadcn/ui.

**Main Application Backend**
- NestJS;
- TypeScript;
- Fastify where appropriate.

**Educational / Intelligence Backend**
- Python;
- FastAPI;
- Pydantic.

**Agent Layer**
- PydanticAI;
- LangGraph where appropriate.

**Database**
- PostgreSQL;
- pgvector.

**Cache**
- Redis.

**Durable Workflows**
- Temporal.

**Object Storage**
- S3-compatible storage.

**Programming**
- Monaco Editor;
- isolated code execution infrastructure.

**Observability**
- OpenTelemetry;
- Sentry.

**Engineering**
- GitHub;
- GitHub Actions;
- pnpm;
- Turborepo.

**Containers**
- Docker;
- Docker Compose.

Use appropriate stable versions. Do not add technologies without a real reason.

## 172. Stack Principles

Every technology must have a clear responsibility.

Prefer:

**Modular Monolith + Specialized Intelligence Layer + Durable Workers**

over many microservices from day one.

## 173. Frontend Architecture

Use Next.js, React, and TypeScript for the learner and teacher applications.

Keep authoritative learning state on the backend.

The frontend should handle:

- user interaction;
- temporary UI state;
- local editing;
- optimistic updates where appropriate.

Do not move the core educational logic into the browser.

## 174. UI Stack

Use Tailwind CSS and shadcn/ui for a consistent component system.

Avoid excessive component duplication, giant page components, and unnecessary styling complexity.

## 175. Main Backend

Use NestJS and TypeScript for:

- authentication;
- authorization;
- users;
- students;
- teachers;
- goals;
- courses;
- roadmaps;
- tasks;
- projects;
- content;
- permissions;
- application APIs.

## 176. Python Intelligence Layer

Use Python and FastAPI for:

- knowledge tracing;
- adaptive assessment;
- recommendations;
- learning analytics;
- forecasting;
- content intelligence;
- educational data processing;
- experimental learning models;
- advanced AI operations.

## 177. Typed Contracts

Use Pydantic for:

- request validation;
- structured AI output;
- agent contracts;
- internal schemas;
- validation of learning decisions.

Use OpenAPI, JSON Schema, typed DTOs, or versioned contracts to coordinate TypeScript and Python.

## 178. Modular Monolith First

Do not immediately split every domain into a separate microservice.

Start with clear module boundaries and extract services only when demonstrated scale or reliability needs justify it.

## 179. PostgreSQL

Use PostgreSQL as the primary source of truth for application and educational state.

Potential entities include:

- users;
- students;
- teachers;
- goals;
- domains;
- concepts;
- skills;
- skill relationships;
- competency states;
- misconceptions;
- curricula;
- roadmaps;
- content items;
- content versions;
- tasks;
- task families;
- attempts;
- hints;
- assessments;
- projects;
- evidence;
- interventions;
- recommendations;
- learning events;
- agent runs;
- providers;
- models;
- experiments.

## 180. PostgreSQL as Source of Truth

Do not make Redis, vector storage, AI providers, agent memory, or browser storage the authoritative source of student learning state.

## 181. pgvector

Use pgvector for semantic retrieval where useful:

- content retrieval;
- similar tasks;
- related concepts;
- resource discovery;
- contextual retrieval.

Do not add a separate vector database without a real requirement.

## 182. Search

Use PostgreSQL structured queries and full-text search alongside pgvector.

Use hybrid retrieval where it improves educational relevance.

## 183. Redis

Use Redis for cache, locks, rate limits, short-lived state, and temporary coordination.

Do not use Redis as the durable Student Learning Twin store.

## 184. Temporal

Use Temporal for durable long-running workflows.

Examples include:

- large content ingestion;
- curriculum generation;
- enrichment pipelines;
- durable approval workflows;
- long-running assessment;
- background processing.

## 185. S3-Compatible Storage

Use object storage for:

- PDFs;
- DOCX;
- EPUB;
- images;
- datasets;
- project files;
- generated artifacts.

Keep metadata and relationships in PostgreSQL.

## 186. Secure Code Execution

Use an isolated execution environment with CPU, memory, time, process, filesystem, and network restrictions.

Never run untrusted student code inside the primary application process.

## 187. Observability

Use OpenTelemetry for traces, metrics, and logs.

Use Sentry for application errors where appropriate.

Trace meaningful learning flows:

`User Action → API → Agent → Tool → Verification → Evidence → State Update → Recommendation`.

## 188. GitHub

Use GitHub as the engineering source of truth for:

- source code;
- schemas;
- migrations;
- tests;
- documentation;
- prompts;
- agent definitions;
- ADRs;
- safe evaluation datasets;
- configuration templates;
- CI/CD definitions.

## 189. Never Commit Secrets

Never commit API keys, passwords, access tokens, credentials, or private learner data.

## 190. Runtime Logging

Keep runtime logs, student event streams, model traces, and private educational data separate from source control.

## 191. Provider Abstraction

Separate:

**Educational Core**
- learner model;
- competencies;
- knowledge graph;
- curriculum;
- evidence;
- tasks;
- assessments.

**AI Orchestration**
- agents;
- routing;
- tool use;
- structured AI operations.

**Provider Adapter**
- protocol;
- endpoint;
- authentication;
- model interface;
- provider-specific capabilities.

**Credentials**
- API keys;
- secrets;
- environment configuration.

## 192. AI Provider Independence

The product must not depend architecturally on DeepSeek, OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, local models, or any single provider.

Any may be supported, but none is the foundation.

## 193. No Provider-Specific Core Logic

Provider-specific logic belongs inside provider adapters, not across educational business logic.

## 194. Model Agnosticism

Treat models as capability profiles rather than hardcoded model names.

Capabilities may include:

- generation;
- coding;
- reasoning;
- vision;
- audio;
- structured output;
- tool use;
- long context.

## 195. Model Routing

Choose models based on task, capability, quality, cost, latency, and availability.

## 196. API Key Independence

Do not assume one permanent global API key.

Support configurable credentials for different environments and, where appropriate, organizations.

Changing credentials must not change educational state.

## 197. Provider Failure

If AI is unavailable:

- preserve state;
- preserve evidence;
- retry appropriately;
- use fallback when configured;
- surface failures;
- do not fabricate successful output.

## 198. Provider Migration

Changing providers must not require rebuilding learner history, competencies, curriculum, or evidence.

## 199. AI Capability Detection

Do not assume all providers support all capabilities.

Use capability detection and graceful fallback.

## 200. Structured AI Output

Where AI output affects application behavior, use validated schemas.

Example:

```python
class LearningDecision(BaseModel):
    action: str
    skill_id: str
    confidence: float
    rationale: str
    evidence_ids: list[str]
```

Do not allow unrestricted free-form text to directly mutate critical state.

## 201. AI Verification

Verify important AI outputs with schemas, deterministic tools, tests, source grounding, rules, secondary evaluation, or human review.

## 202. Cost Control

Use expensive AI calls only when they provide meaningful value.

Use deterministic logic where it is sufficient.

## 203. Latency Control

Avoid unnecessary multi-agent chains.

Prefer one useful AI operation plus verification over many agents interacting without measurable benefit.

---

# PART XII — APPLICATION DATA, SECURITY AND GOVERNANCE

## 204. Data Model

Potential entities include:

- User;
- Student;
- Teacher;
- Goal;
- Domain;
- Concept;
- Skill;
- Skill Relationship;
- Competency State;
- Curriculum;
- Roadmap;
- Module;
- Lesson;
- Content Item;
- Content Version;
- Source;
- Misconception;
- Task;
- Task Family;
- Attempt;
- Hint;
- Assessment;
- Project;
- Evidence;
- Learning Event;
- Intervention;
- Recommendation;
- Agent;
- Agent Run;
- Provider;
- Model;
- Credential Configuration;
- Experiment.

Adapt entity names to the actual implementation rather than forcing every proposed entity.

## 205. Data Separation

Separate:

- application data;
- learning state;
- educational content;
- runtime logs;
- AI traces;
- credentials.

## 206. Event System

Record meaningful events such as:

- `task_started`;
- `task_completed`;
- `task_failed`;
- `hint_requested`;
- `hint_used`;
- `code_executed`;
- `test_passed`;
- `test_failed`;
- `confidence_submitted`;
- `explanation_submitted`;
- `misconception_detected`;
- `misconception_retested`;
- `retention_checked`;
- `transfer_completed`;
- `mastery_updated`;
- `roadmap_updated`;
- `content_generated`;
- `content_enriched`.

## 207. Event Provenance

Events should contain enough context to understand what happened:

- learner;
- skill;
- task;
- time;
- support;
- result;
- verification;
- consequence.

## 208. Analytics

Analytics should support:

**Observation → Interpretation → Intervention → Outcome.**

Do not build analytics solely for visualization.

## 209. Learning Metrics

Primary metrics:

- mastery gain;
- retention;
- transfer;
- independent performance;
- reduction in hint dependence;
- improvement after intervention;
- project competence.

Secondary metrics may include completion, engagement, and satisfaction.

## 210. Content Metrics

Evaluate content through learning gain, error reduction, transfer, retention, and independence.

## 211. Recommendation Metrics

Evaluate whether recommended actions produce meaningful learning outcomes.

## 212. Synthetic Learners

Create synthetic learner profiles for testing, including:

- beginner;
- false-fluent learner;
- hint-dependent learner;
- misconception-heavy learner;
- advanced learner;
- forgetting learner.

## 213. Educational Regression Tests

Verify that:

- evidence updates mastery appropriately;
- unsupported mastery cannot appear from nowhere;
- hint dependence affects independence;
- misconceptions trigger interventions;
- transfer remains distinct from repetition;
- forgetting triggers review;
- generated content retains provenance;
- provider changes preserve learner state.

## 214. AI Regression Tests

Test:

- no inappropriate answer leakage;
- rubric compliance;
- appropriate uncertainty;
- no fake verification;
- no fabricated source claims;
- correct permission handling.

## 215. Provider Regression

Where practical, run educational behavior tests across multiple providers.

Exact wording may differ, but core educational behavior should remain within acceptable bounds.

## 216. Content Regression

Verify that generated content:

- maps to intended competencies;
- does not replace canonical material;
- respects enrichment settings;
- preserves provenance;
- passes validation.

## 217. Failure-First Design

Test:

- AI unavailable;
- malformed AI response;
- provider authentication failure;
- rate limit;
- tool failure;
- code execution failure;
- invalid generated content;
- conflicting evidence;
- missing prerequisite;
- empty material library;
- incomplete curriculum.

Fail safely.

## 218. Privacy

Use:

- least privilege;
- access control;
- encryption;
- data minimization;
- retention policies;
- auditability.

## 219. Security

Protect:

- student data;
- teacher data;
- credentials;
- code execution;
- assessment integrity;
- internal infrastructure.

## 220. Accessibility

Support keyboard navigation, screen readers, readable typography, clear focus states, captions, transcripts, alternative representations, and low-distraction modes.

## 221. Fairness

Monitor meaningful systematic differences in assessment, support, task assignment, and recommendations.

Avoid unnecessary inference of sensitive traits.

## 222. Human Governance

Teachers and administrators should be able to inspect, correct, approve, disable, or override important system behavior.

## 223. Auditability

Important changes should record who changed what, when, and the previous and new state.

---

# PART XIII — UI / UX DESIGN DIRECTION

## 224. Product Design Philosophy

The interface should feel like a serious intelligent learning environment, not a traditional LMS.

The visual direction should combine:

- modern developer tools;
- premium productivity software;
- intelligent research environments;
- minimal academic interfaces.

The product should feel intelligent, calm, precise, modern, focused, and trustworthy.

## 225. Visual Principles

Prefer:

- strong typography;
- generous whitespace;
- subtle borders;
- restrained shadows;
- clear hierarchy;
- compact but readable controls;
- meaningful color accents;
- contextual actions;
- responsive layouts;
- keyboard-friendly interactions.

Avoid:

- childish educational aesthetics;
- excessive gamification;
- excessive gradients;
- large decorative illustrations;
- visual clutter;
- excessive cards;
- meaningless dashboards;
- overly colorful interfaces;
- constant AI chat panels.

## 226. Main Screen

The home screen should emphasize:

- current goal;
- current learning state;
- next best action;
- important reviews;
- unresolved bottlenecks.

The primary question should be:

**“What should I do next?”**

not:

“How many lessons have I completed?”

## 227. Learning Workspace

The interface should adapt to the current activity.

Reading may use a focused content layout.

Programming should use an editor and testing workspace.

Assessment should reduce distractions.

Projects should have milestones and artifacts.

Reflection should emphasize learner-authored responses.

## 228. Knowledge Map

The knowledge map must be functional, not decorative.

It should help answer:

- What do I know?
- What is weak?
- What blocks me?
- What comes next?

A selected skill may show mastery, independence, retention, transfer, evidence, and next recommended action.

## 229. Error Center

Group recurring errors by:

- skill;
- misconception;
- frequency;
- severity;
- recency;
- intervention status.

## 230. Materials UI

Show:

- original material;
- generated material;
- enriched material;
- source;
- provenance;
- validation;
- approval status;
- related competencies.

## 231. Student Control

Students should be able to see recommendations, understand them, influence goals, request help, and choose among reasonable alternatives.

## 232. Adaptive UI

The visual scaffolding itself should be able to decrease as learner expertise increases.

Beginners may receive more structure and clearer guidance.

Advanced learners may receive a cleaner workspace, fewer prompts, and more room for independent work.

---

# PART XIV — DEVELOPMENT, TESTING AND DELIVERY

## 233. Modular Monolith First

Do not begin with dozens of microservices.

Start with clear module boundaries and extract services only when actual scale or reliability needs justify it.

## 234. Development Order

Build around working vertical slices.

A useful sequence is:

1. repository and application foundation;
2. authentication and user identity;
3. core domain models;
4. skill and concept graph;
5. Student Learning Twin;
6. task and attempt system;
7. Evidence Engine;
8. diagnostic assessment;
9. personalized roadmap;
10. basic tutoring;
11. cold-start content generation;
12. programming execution;
13. task validation;
14. content ingestion;
15. material enrichment;
16. adaptive interventions;
17. advanced agents.

## 235. Vertical Slice Method

For each important feature:

**Domain Model → Service Logic → API → UI → Evidence → Tests.**

## 236. MVP Objective

The MVP must prove one thing:

**The system can understand a learner well enough to provide a better next learning action than a static course sequence.**

## 237. MVP Must Work Without Materials

The MVP must support both:

- material-based learning;
- goal-based cold-start learning.

## 238. MVP Learning Loop

Implement:

`Goal → Diagnosis → Roadmap → Task → Attempt → Evidence → Next Action`.

## 239. MVP Programming Loop

Implement:

`Task → Code → Execute → Tests → Feedback → Retry → Evidence`.

## 240. MVP Materials Loop

Implement:

`Upload → Analyze → Map to Competencies → Coverage → Gap Detection`.

## 241. MVP Generated Content

Support:

- roadmap generation;
- lesson generation;
- task generation;
- assessment generation;
- targeted explanations;
- hint generation.

## 242. MVP Enrichment

At minimum support:

- enrichment on/off;
- original vs generated content distinction;
- provenance;
- no silent overwrite.

## 243. MVP Personalization Test

At least two learners with different competency states must receive meaningfully different next actions.

If every learner receives the same sequence, the adaptive system is not functioning correctly.

## 244. MVP Content Test

The platform must successfully begin teaching when the content library is empty.

## 245. MVP Provider Test

Switching the configured AI provider must not change the learner's historical educational state.

## 246. MVP Failure Test

AI failure must not destroy:

- learner state;
- tasks;
- content;
- evidence;
- roadmap;
- learning history.

## 247. Phase 2

Add:

- misconception engine;
- retention engine;
- transfer engine;
- advanced code intelligence;
- metacognition;
- project intelligence;
- teacher dashboards;
- content enrichment;
- richer adaptive assessment.

## 248. Phase 3

Add:

- advanced agent orchestration;
- research mode;
- forecasting;
- competency passport;
- additional domains;
- advanced provider routing;
- experimentation;
- sophisticated analytics.

## 249. Avoid Overengineering

Do not begin with Kubernetes, Kafka, multiple databases, dozens of services, or excessive agent infrastructure without a demonstrated need.

Keep clear boundaries so the architecture can evolve later.

## 250. Database Migrations

Use versioned migrations.

Never silently alter production schemas.

## 251. API Versioning

Use stable contracts for important APIs.

## 252. Type Safety

Use strict typing wherever practical in TypeScript, Pydantic schemas, API contracts, and agent outputs.

## 253. Testing Strategy

Use:

**Unit tests** for educational logic and state transitions.

**Integration tests** for services, databases, content pipelines, and AI orchestration.

**End-to-end tests** for complete learner journeys.

**AI behavioral tests** for agents, tutors, evaluators, and content generators.

## 254. Prompt Versioning

Treat critical prompts as versioned software artifacts.

Track:

- prompt ID;
- version;
- purpose;
- model capability assumptions;
- evaluation;
- changelog.

## 255. Agent Versioning

Track agent role, tools, permissions, prompt, version, expected outputs, and evaluations.

## 256. Architecture Decision Records

Use ADRs for important decisions, including:

- provider abstraction;
- learning-state architecture;
- content provenance;
- enrichment behavior;
- competency model;
- assessment architecture.

## 257. Feature Flags

Use feature flags where useful for experimental learning strategies, enrichment, advanced agents, model routing, UI experiments, and new assessment modes.

## 258. CI/CD

Use GitHub Actions for:

- lint;
- type checking;
- tests;
- migration validation;
- builds;
- security checks;
- deployment pipelines.

## 259. Local Development

A developer should be able to run the project locally using documented steps.

Use Docker Compose for local infrastructure where practical.

## 260. Environment Separation

Support:

- development;
- staging;
- production.

## 261. Configuration

Do not hardcode credentials, provider URLs, model names, or environment-specific settings.

## 262. Observability of AI

Capture appropriate metadata:

- provider;
- model;
- operation;
- latency;
- failure;
- verification status.

Never expose secrets.

## 263. Performance

Optimize after measuring.

Potential mechanisms include:

- indexing;
- caching;
- batching;
- asynchronous jobs;
- model routing;
- efficient retrieval.

## 264. Cost

Use expensive AI only when it provides meaningful value.

Prefer deterministic logic when sufficient.

## 265. Latency

Avoid unnecessary AI chains.

Prefer one useful AI operation plus verification over many agents interacting without measurable benefit.

---

# PART XV — NON-NEGOTIABLE ARCHITECTURAL TESTS

## 266. Provider Change Test

If the current AI provider disappears, the platform must retain:

- learner state;
- competencies;
- evidence;
- curriculum;
- content;
- project history.

## 267. Content Change Test

If materials are removed or replaced:

- learner state remains;
- competency history remains;
- evidence remains;
- the roadmap is recalculated rather than destroyed.

## 268. Goal Change Test

If the learner changes goals, preserve learning history, recalculate target competencies, and reuse mastered knowledge where relevant.

## 269. Model Change Test

If the AI model changes, preserve learning state, content provenance, assessments, prompt versions, and provider metadata.

## 270. Failure Resilience Test

An AI outage must not destroy learner data, learning history, competency state, curriculum, or original materials.

## 271. Educational Trust Test

A user must be able to distinguish:

- what is observed;
- what is inferred;
- what was generated;
- what was validated;
- what is approved;
- what is uncertain.

## 272. Product Trust Test

The system must not silently:

- change learning goals;
- overwrite original content;
- invent mastery;
- fabricate verification;
- claim an agent succeeded when a tool failed.

---

# PART XVI — GOLDEN LOOPS

## 273. Golden Learning Loop

```text
LEARNER GOAL
    ↓
TARGET OUTCOME
    ↓
COMPETENCY GRAPH
    ↓
CURRENT LEARNER STATE
    ↓
DIAGNOSIS
    ↓
PERSONALIZED ROADMAP
    ↓
CONTENT COVERAGE
    ↓
MISSING CONTENT / MATERIAL GAPS
    ↓
CONTENT SELECTION OR GENERATION
    ↓
LEARNING ACTION
    ↓
ATTEMPT
    ↓
VERIFICATION
    ↓
EVIDENCE
    ↓
STUDENT LEARNING TWIN UPDATE
    ↓
MISCONCEPTION / RETENTION / TRANSFER / INDEPENDENCE ANALYSIS
    ↓
INTERVENTION
    ↓
NEW ATTEMPT
    ↓
INDEPENDENT PERFORMANCE
    ↓
MASTERY
    ↓
RETENTION
    ↓
TRANSFER
    ↓
NEXT COMPETENCY
```

## 274. Cold-Start Loop

```text
USER GOAL
    ↓
DOMAIN IDENTIFICATION
    ↓
TARGET LEVEL
    ↓
COMPETENCY MAP
    ↓
DIAGNOSTIC
    ↓
PERSONALIZED ROADMAP
    ↓
GENERATED INITIAL CONTENT
    ↓
PRACTICE
    ↓
ASSESSMENT
    ↓
EVIDENCE
    ↓
ADAPTATION
```

## 275. Content Enrichment Loop

```text
AVAILABLE MATERIALS
    ↓
CURRICULUM REQUIREMENTS
    ↓
COVERAGE ANALYSIS
    ↓
MISSING KNOWLEDGE
    ↓
ENRICHMENT POLICY
    ↓
GENERATE / RETRIEVE / SUGGEST
    ↓
VALIDATE
    ↓
PRESERVE PROVENANCE
    ↓
PERSIST IF ALLOWED
    ↓
USE IN LEARNING
    ↓
MEASURE OUTCOME
    ↓
IMPROVE CONTENT
```

## 276. AI Interaction Loop

```text
LEARNER MESSAGE
    ↓
INTENT DETECTION
    ↓
CONTEXT BUILDER
    ↓
STUDENT LEARNING TWIN
    ↓
KNOWLEDGE GRAPH
    ↓
CURRENT OBJECTIVE / TASK
    ↓
AI ORCHESTRATOR
    ↓
SPECIALIZED AGENT
    ↓
PROVIDER ADAPTER
    ↓
AI MODEL
    ↓
STRUCTURED OUTPUT
    ↓
VALIDATION / TOOL USE
    ↓
RESPONSE OR EDUCATIONAL ACTION
    ↓
LEARNER RESPONSE
    ↓
EVIDENCE
    ↓
LEARNING STATE UPDATE
    ↓
NEXT BEST ACTION
```

## 277. Task Generation Loop

```text
LEARNER STATE
    ↓
GOAL
    ↓
TARGET COMPETENCY
    ↓
SKILL SELECTION
    ↓
TASK FAMILY
    ↓
TASK SPECIFICATION
    ↓
DIFFICULTY
    ↓
COGNITIVE LEVEL
    ↓
MISCONCEPTION TARGET
    ↓
INDEPENDENCE REQUIREMENT
    ↓
TRANSFER REQUIREMENT
    ↓
EXISTING TASK SEARCH
    ↓
REUSE / MUTATE / GENERATE
    ↓
VALIDATION
    ↓
DETERMINISTIC VERIFICATION
    ↓
APPROVAL
    ↓
STUDENT ATTEMPT
    ↓
EVIDENCE
    ↓
LEARNING STATE UPDATE
```

## 278. Agent Loop

```text
OBSERVE
    ↓
ANALYZE
    ↓
SELECT ACTION
    ↓
USE TOOL
    ↓
VERIFY
    ↓
CREATE EVIDENCE
    ↓
UPDATE LEARNING STATE
    ↓
FINISH / ESCALATE
```

## 279. Engineering Loop

```text
REQUIREMENT
    ↓
DOMAIN MODEL
    ↓
IMPLEMENTATION
    ↓
API
    ↓
UI
    ↓
EVIDENCE
    ↓
TEST
    ↓
OBSERVE
    ↓
ITERATE
```

---

# PART XVII — DEFINITION OF DONE

## 280. Feature Completion

A feature is not complete merely because:

- a page exists;
- a button exists;
- an API exists;
- an AI response appears;
- a mockup looks good.

A feature is complete only when it:

- works in the real product;
- uses the correct data model;
- participates in the learning loop where relevant;
- creates appropriate evidence;
- updates state correctly;
- handles failures;
- is tested;
- is observable;
- respects permissions;
- respects privacy;
- preserves provenance;
- remains provider-independent where appropriate.

## 281. Implementation Priority

When choosing what to build next, prioritize:

**Educational Correctness → Reliability → Simplicity → Extensibility → Performance Optimization.**

## 282. Build from the Learning Loop

Do not start with beautiful dashboards, a giant AI chat interface, or dozens of settings.

Start with the learning loop and evidence model.

## 283. Do Not Build from the AI Outward

Do not start by asking which model should power everything.

Start by asking:

**What educational decision must the system make?**

Then determine whether AI is necessary.

## 284. No Hidden Magic

Never implement an opaque pipeline:

`AI → Mysterious Score → Mysterious Recommendation`.

Prefer:

`Evidence → State → Decision → Action → Outcome`.

## 285. Final Engineering Questions

Before implementing a feature, ask:

1. What product problem does it solve?
2. What educational problem does it solve?
3. What data does it require?
4. What evidence does it create?
5. Which component owns it?
6. Can it be deterministic?
7. Does AI add real value?
8. What happens if AI fails?
9. Does it preserve provider independence?
10. Does it preserve learning state?
11. How is it tested?
12. How is it observed?

## 286. Final Educational Questions

Before making a learning decision, ask:

1. What does the evidence say?
2. What is uncertain?
3. What is the likely cause of the problem?
4. What is the smallest useful intervention?
5. Should support increase or decrease?
6. Should the learner retrieve, practice, transfer, reflect, or learn?
7. How will improvement be verified?

## 287. Final Material Directive

Never assume materials must exist.

The system must be able to create a useful learning experience from a goal alone.

Never silently modify canonical materials.

Respect the enrichment setting.

Preserve provenance.

Validate important additions.

## 288. Final AI Directive

Never assume one AI provider.

Never hardcode one model.

Never hardcode one API key.

Never make educational state provider-specific.

Use AI where it adds value.

Use deterministic logic where sufficient.

Verify important AI outputs.

Preserve uncertainty.

## 289. Final Agent Directive

Never create an agent without:

- responsibility;
- boundaries;
- tools;
- verification;
- permissions;
- failure handling;
- tests.

Do not use agents to replace ordinary deterministic application logic.

## 290. Final Data Directive

The learner's educational state must remain durable, traceable, explainable, and portable.

## 291. Final Security Directive

Never compromise:

- credentials;
- learner privacy;
- assessment integrity;
- code execution isolation.

## 292. Final Product Outcome

The learner should eventually be able to say:

- I know what I understand.
- I know where my weak points are.
- I understand why I make mistakes.
- I can solve problems without constant help.
- I can apply knowledge in unfamiliar situations.
- I can recover information I have forgotten.
- I can learn independently.

## 293. Final Technology Principle

Use the technology stack to serve the educational model.

Do not let the framework, agent library, database, provider, model, or infrastructure become the product architecture by accident.

Technology is replaceable.

Educational state is not.

## 294. Final Build Directive

Before writing code:

1. Inspect the repository.
2. Understand the current architecture.
3. Identify existing functionality.
4. Identify the data model.
5. Identify reusable components.
6. Identify contradictions.
7. Identify missing abstractions.
8. Avoid unnecessary rewrites.
9. Define the smallest correct vertical slice.
10. Implement it.
11. Test it.
12. Validate the actual learning loop.
13. Document important architectural decisions.

Preserve working functionality. Refactor incrementally where reasonable.

## 295. Final North Star

Build a platform that can start with either:

**“Here are my materials.”**

or:

**“I want to learn this.”**

In both cases, it must be able to:

- understand the goal;
- understand or build the curriculum;
- diagnose the learner;
- generate or use learning content;
- detect missing information;
- optionally enrich materials according to explicit settings;
- generate meaningful tasks;
- assess actual competence;
- identify misconceptions;
- distinguish forgetting from misunderstanding;
- adapt difficulty;
- adapt support;
- use retrieval, spacing, and interleaving;
- test transfer;
- develop metacognition;
- support programming and project work;
- use agents when genuinely useful;
- verify important AI outputs;
- preserve evidence and provenance;
- remain independent of any AI provider;
- preserve learner state across model and provider changes;
- remain secure and observable;
- gradually make the learner less dependent on the system.

**Build the learning intelligence first.**

**Use AI as an amplifier.**

**Use agents as tools.**
